New Delhi, the 8th May, 2000 

S. O* lOilj—Wiici'L'its by a notification of the Govern mem of ind;:! in Ministry of 
Petroleum :ir\d Natural Gas, number S.O. 3l6(i dated 22''"’ October 2007, issued under 
sub-section (1) of Section 3 of the Pctrolcuin and MineriUs Pipelines (Acquisition of 
Right of User in Land) Act, 1952 (50 of 1962) (hereinaher referred to as the said Act), 
the Government of India declared its intention to acquire the Right of User in the land, 
speeiAed in the Schedule appended to that notification for the purpose of laying pipeline 
for transportation of natural gas from structures in Andhra Pradesh of M/s Reliance 
Industries Limited, by M/s Reliance Gas Transportation Infrastructure Limited to various 
consumers ; 

And whereas the copies of the said Gazette notification were made available to the public 
on or before ift”* February, 2008; 

And whereas no objections were received from the public to the laying of the pipeline; 

And whereas the Competent Authority has under sub-scction (I) of Section 6 of the said 
Act, submitted report to the Government of India; 

And whereas the Government of India, after considering the said report and on being 
satisfied that the said land is required for lading the pipeline, has decided to acquire the 
Rjg^t of User therein; 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by sub-section (1) of Section 6 of the 
said Act, the Government of India hereby declares that the Right of User in the land, 
specified in the Schedule, appended to this notification, is hereby acquired for laying the 
pipeline; 

And fiirther, in exercise of the powers conferred by sub-scction (4) of Section 6 of the 
said Act, the Government of India hereby directs that the Right of User in the said land 
for laying the pipeline shall, instead of vesting in the Government of India, vest on the 
date of publication of the declaration, in M/s Reliance Gas Transportation Infrastructure 
Limited, free from all encumbrances.

fixed by the railway administration alter accepting the 
prescribed amount directly fi^om the passengers. The railway 
administration, provides them unironn and Badge who are 
popularly known as coolie. They are not the workman of 
the railways but foe Ifoensees paying to ^ railway while 
the parcel porters .are the workman getting salary or 
particular amount as remuneration, may be on daily basis^ 
fobm railway. The work of foe parcel porters is to cany foe 
parcels from and to foe ofOce and load and unload it in the 
wagons. Thus they do the work of luiJoading'also and 
canying the parcels from office the wagpo to the office u 
per directions of foe railway administration for which they 
are paid fay railway. 

9. If the evidence is scanned and tested, on the 
above basis, nb body will dare lo say that they are not (he 
workman of the railway. They were appointed by calling 
applications, after screening tests and interviews. The 
petitioner’s witness, who was foe member of the selection 
cenunittee, has admitted to that effect. They were never 
given badges, buckles and uniform. Ibeir presence was 
marked in the Muster roll. Initially foey were given four 
hours duty later on it was increased . They were woiking 
under direct control bf the Railway adminbiration. Similar 
thing regarding the work they wm asked to perfonn the 
work like handling foe paroels inclu^ng loading unloading 
and even opening end resealing the wagons. The Most 
important and undisputed thing is that foe Railway was 
paying wages to them on monthly basis through 
supervisors, station masters instate of recovering the 
license fee from them. They were paid as per Minimum 
Wagies AcL In case of licensed porters there is uo question 
Of paying any remuneration by Railway. No evidence is 
produced to ^ow that petitioners havepaida license fee. 
Beside it there are exajrqiles of even effecting foetrttansfers 
by the railway which can not be a event in c^ of licensees. 
In fact foe stand of the respondenl managemenl is not 
certain about the work provided to foe petitioners but it is 
seems undisputed that (hi^ were handling parcels. Had 
they been appointed as licensee of coolies they were not 
even entitled to touch the parcels. No doubt they have 
signed the agreements separately but they will not prove 
them as licensee. On the contrary foey are incomplete and 
seem to have obtained only to avoid their rights. They 
were provided with identity cards os parcel Porters and 
experience certifleates . It is contend^ on behalf of foe 
management that the certificates art not issued by its 
incompetent officers, alleging thereby that they are bogus. 
But no evidence is adduced to show (hat any action has 
been taken against erring officer, even their names are not 
declared mui^ Less about their incompetence. In fact for 
issuing licenses to foe coolies there was* no need of

as coolies as claimed by foe managcmeiit.

10« Ati Attempt has been made ia the evidence by 
Sliri Murtbi to show that to the petitioners were favoured 
by dip management by asking them to work as parcels 
pQrten^ However it is not the case or the defence of the 
Management, There would ag^in a question as to how the 
discrimiaation was made leaving other coolies. Another 
attempt is made by the manageineiit with a view to avoid 
the liability by submitting that they were rtot working as 
causal labours, therefore they are not entitled for 
regitla(tlzation. I have already concluded that they are the 
workmen of railway and have completed 240 days 
contiipiotis service. The management has admitted that 
the petitioners were paid the wages as per notification of 
the Labour Ministry. If they were licensee what was the 
reason for paying them. One more theory is introduced 
that they were absconding and Uierefore their services 
were terminated, in one breath it is saying that they were 
not then- workmen and in another breath h is contending 
that Ibc^ were terminated. If they were not the workmen 
then bow they were terminated is the question, U falsifies 
the stand of Railway Management, 

] 1. It appears that in 1994 Raitway has started the 
lectuilinieflto fParcel Porters by issuiiigspecial notification 
after cancelling the earlier practice of taking the work of 
panel potters through private contractors. However when 
the petitioners raised demand for job security, the Railway 
Administration started harassing them. The evidence 
clearly shows that they were appointed as parcel porters 
and were workman of the railway ri^ from the day of their 
respective appointiueius^ They are aippointed in the year 
1994.. 1995 and undisputedly each of them have completed 
nuHv than 240 days. The nature of their work is purely 
mmiiflll and unskilled. They will have to be treated as 
wptfoiien as defuied u/s 2 (s) of I.D. Act 1947. 

12. It seems the petitioner Union has claimed 60 and 
151 as Older Nos. L ^101 l/ZS/97-IR(B DDtd. 2S-0e '199S 
and Order No. L-4I011/9/99-IR (B-l) Dtd. 16-08-1999 
respectively total 211 parcel porters are emitied for the 
regularization. Even the above lefened orders ofthe Central 
Oovt of India has also given the similar number of the 
parcel porters. The list of the 60 petitioners in the first 
order is in confennity of number given by the petitioner. 
However the actual number as per appended lists with the 
OrderNo-L^lOl 1)9W-1R(B-I)D«1,16-08-1999 islEssby9 
parcel porters it does not confirm the claimed number of 
131potters. It comes 143 only totalling to 203 in all. The 
petitioners have given the lists of the porters woiking at 
parllcular stations as under:— 

(l)Kainih] Rly. Station, 16 paicel potters, (2) Bhandara 
Road (]9,(3)Tumsar road 11, (4) Tiroda 04, (5) Condiya 59, 
(6) Riynandgaon 08 and (7) Itwari Rly Station 36 parcel 


porters. Thus the total comes to 143 porters besides the 60 
porters/petitioners in reference No. 203/1998 makbiggrand 
total of203 parcel porters. 

13. The evidence is clear enough to show that the 
petitioners were regularly working and the work is also of 
perennial nature, Since they have completed more than 240 
days they are entitle to regularization. No doubt they were 
provided with the work for 4 hours on each day and some 
times more than it but it was deliberate attempt to deprive 
them From regularization. It is not at all acase ofthe railway 
that their appointment was of some other cadre, b my view 
though they were working 4 hours or some times for 8 
hours they were oti daily wages and are entitled for 
regularization and declamtion that they are (he employees 
of rail ways. 

14, Soferasbackwagesare coitcem the petitioners 
have worked as per directions ofthe authoriiiy and they are 
already paid as per their working hours. If Is pertinent to 
note that in remaining time they were not prohibited to do 
their work. Tn fact they were working m addition to the 
regularly appointed parcel ponm hence in my view they 
are not entitled to back wages as prayed. I proceed to pass 
the following order.

The respondent party No. 2 The Divisional

parcel porters as per their qualificatiDn and 
suitability, treating them as railway employee. 

1 To fix their pay in the above regular cadre from 
the date of their respective appointments and 
giving the regular increments pay the salary 
with prospective effect from the date of 
noPficatlon of this award, 

3. The lists of 60 and 143 parcel potters appended 
along with the above numbwed orders of the 
Ministry shall form part and preel of this 
award.

1. Central Covenuneat, MOL, New Delhi, vide

referred (he following dispute to this Tribunal for

Whether the action of Ibe management of Allahabad 
Bank Kanpur in dismissing ShriNatwar Lai Tandon 
clerk from the service of the bank w.e.f25-08-94 is 
legal and justified? If not what relief is the said 
workman entitled? 

2. The case of the union on behalfof the workman as 
setup in the statement of claim in short is that a fraud is 


alleged to have been oommitted at PheeUdiana Branch of 
the Bank at Kanpur in which the Manager and Assistant 
Branch Manager viz Sri K. L, Tandon and R, R. Bajpai 
respectively were reportedly involved and both the officers 
named above ere under suspension frotn the bank and are 
on bail where after neither any action against them has 
been initiated by the bank nor by the C.B.I. which has been 
investigating the case so tar. The manager remained posted 
at the branch for about six years against the term of three 
years prescribed in the bank for theii pasting at apkee for 
the officers including managers and after that he was 
transferred to LajpatNagar Branch of the Bank at Kanpur 
and in a couple of months he was again posted at 
Pheelkhana Branch of the Bank. In the same fashion Sri 
Bajpai the Assistant Manager was transferred to Rania 
Branch of the Bank and thereafter he was again allowed to 
be posted at Pheelkhana Branch of the Bank. The manner 
in which both the officers of the bank were retained at 
Pheelkhana BranrS proves that the Assistant Genera! 
Manager, Kanpur and the Regioud Manager of the branch 
were mvolvedneck deep in the alleged baud The Fegfonal 
Manager of the bank untbrtiinateJy happened to be the 
disclplmaiy authority in the case of the workmari and the 
Assistant General Manager also happened to be the 
appellate authority and both of them remaitied silent so far 
relating to in itiatlon of disciplinary action against the Branch 
Manager and Ibe Assistant Manager of Pheelkhana Brunch 
of the bank but preferred to proceed against the workman 
and some other employees of the bank. The workman was 
placed under suspension by the bank on 17-3'94 and later 
on served with a charge sheet dated 39-05-94, by the 
Regional Manager / DiscipLinaty Authority,

depaitmenial inquity was ordered by the dLscipJinary

rule is that of reinstatement with back wages as the 
Hon^ble Supreme Court in number of coses has laid down 
about the legal principles to be applied for such cases by 
taking aptogmalic view in die matter and by keeping number 
of foctors in mind viz. that an industiy m^ not be compelled 
to pay to (he worieman for (be period during which he 
apparently contributed a little or nbdiing a( gU. It has been

v4u> bad worked for a sihciFt period, i,e., less than a year or

bock wages were denied to him although the termination of 
his service was found to have been made in violation of 
SectUnt25F of the Act The Hon'ble Apex Court observed 
that the principles to be considered for swarding bock 
wages or cornpansaiion etc- is to be guided by number 
of tactors to be considered for the same. Tt was observed 
that: 

"There is no rule of thumb that in every case where 
the Industrial Tribunal gives a finding that the 
tereiinaticn of service was in violation of Section 
25^ ofihe Act, entire back wages should be awarded. 
A host of fisciors like the manner and method of 
seJection and appointment, Le,, whellier after proper 
adveitisenieiit ofihe vacancy or inviting applicatinns 
from the Employment Exchange,' nature of 


ap^htiment, namely, whether ad hoc; short tann, 
daily wage, tempmmy or pernunetit In character, arty 
special qualifk^ion required for tire job and the like 
should be weighed and balanced in taking a decision 
ngardifig award of back wages. One ofihe importatrt 
Actors, which has to be taken into consideration, is 
the length of service, which the workman had 
rendered with the employer if the workman has 
rendered a oonsideroble pe. iud of service and his 
urvices are wrongftilly terminated, he may be 
awarded foil or partial bsckpwiges keepiiig in view 
the fact that at his age and the qualification 
possessed by him he may not be in a poshion to get 
another ernploynient. However, where the total kogth 
of service rendered by a workman is very small, the 
award of back wages for the complete period, i.e., 
from the dateof teiminatioD dU the date of the award, 
which our experience shows is often quite large, 
would 'be inappropriate. Another importarK fttftor, 
which requires to be taken into consideration Is the 
nature of employment, A regtikr service of 
petmanent character cannot be compared to short or 
intermittent daily wage employnvent (bough it 
be for 240 days in a calendar year." 

In Allahabad isl Ranathan V. Days Shankar itai, 
{3005)5 see 124:2005 AlRSCW3«6aflKT«m:itiering(he 
relevant cases on the point the Hon'ble Apex Court 
staled: 

“We hove reftred to certain decisions of this Court 
to highlight that earlier in the event of on order of 
dismissal being set aside, rebistotement with foil 
back-wages was the usual resuh. But now with the 
passage of tune, it has come to be realized that 
indushy is belpg compelled to pay the workman for 
a period during which he apparently contributed little 
or nothing ai nil, for a period that was spent 
unproductively, while the workman is being 
competled to go bock to a situation which prevailed 
many years ago when he was dismissed. It is 
necessary ibr us'to develop a pngmatic approach to 
problems dqgglrtg industria] rebtions. However no 
just solution can be offered but the ^Iden mean 
may be arrived aL”

"43. Thus, h is clear that adherent ^ to the rule of 
equality in public employment is a basic feature of 
GUT Constitution and since the rule af law is the core 
of our Constitution, a court would certainly be 
disabled from passing an order upholding a violatibn 
of Anicle 14 or In ordering the overlooking of the 
need to comply with the requirements of Article 14 
read with Anicle 16 of the ConstftutionH Therefore, 
oonsistcni with the scheme fhr public employment, 
this Court while laying down the law* has necessarily 
to hold that unless the appointment is in terms of the 
relevant rules and after a proper competitian among 
qualified persons, the same would not confer any 
right on the appointee. If it Is a contractual 
appointment, the appointment comes to an end at 
the end of the contract, if it were an engagement or 
appointment on daily wages or casual basis^ the same 
would come to an end when it is discontinuEfd. 
Similarly, a temporary employee could not claim to 
be made permanent on the expiry of his term of 
appoinimentH It has also to be clarified that merely 
because a temporary employee or a casual wage 
worker i£ continued for a time beyond the tem of his 
appointment, he would not be entitled to be absorbed 
in regular service or made permanent merely on the 
Strength of such continuance, if the original 
afjpoiaimenl was not made by following a due 
process of selection as envisaged by the relevant 
rules. It is not open to the Court to prevent regular 
recruitment at the instance of temporary employees 
whose period of employment has come to an end or 
of an ad hoc employees who by the very uature of 
their appointment, do nor acquire any right."'

Heard argumem irom both the sides and peruEcd the

that he was engaged as Security Guard on ] O-Oft- 19fi9 to

It was submitted from the side of the management

. wcjie lerminated aftor conducting proper and fair inquiry.

aimexed with the record. It-oppear^ that summary inquity 
was made^ The workman was not given opportunity of 
personal hearing and he was not given opportunity to 
cross^amiue the witnesses and to adduce evidence in 
defcDce. Thus, the inquiry is iK>t fair and proper. 

It was further submitted from the side of the

icfeired the following dispute for the adjudicaffen of the

this Tribunal and was negistened on P-9-2005; 

^'Whether the activities of the Equine Breeding 
Stud, HissATp constimte to be that of an industry

Managemetit of Equine Breeding Stud, Hissar in

workman is just and fair? If not, to what relief the 
workinan concerned is entitled to?'' 

On getting (be notice the parties aippcared through 
Iheff representative and counsels and filed their pleadings

peraons they wished to examine as their witnesses in the

gothis statement recorded, but he wanted to produce more

of evidence available on record. 

Tbe claim of the workman is that the Managenuem 
form is an industry as downed by the Industrial Disputes

for the production of higjior quality of horses for the Anny,

aloi^ wjfo co-workers raised a coiid^u^ claim for Minim 
wages to the Management in March, 1990. The 
instead of Mhnnuunwag^ 

■D the workmen who had raised the claim by a verbal order 
dated 8(h of March, 1990, b violation of law and rules. The 
Management later on granted minimum wage^ to the 
workers when they put up (heir claim for that before the 
ambedties. The Management further violated the law and 
rules by not preparing the lists of workers before 
diaengaiging them, by following the principle of first come 
last go. According to the workman, his termination from 
services is bad in law^ therefore, the same is required to be 
quashed and the workman be treated m servics undbe also 
given Rgulaiization in service and grade under rules. 

The management has opposed the claim of the

In support of his claim the workman appeared as

AS Sewennmi. The managemcTit witness has also admitted 
thaibe was CR^gcd on daily wages and he was being paid

It is settLcd Law that even the contractual workers 
engaged tfmnjgh contractors for peretmial nature of work^ 
becomes the employee of the PrincipaJ Employer, in case 
coiUract is sham and ruse and in case it is found that a 
wodcman is working under control and supervision of the 
management and his services are integrated to the 
managenKiit and payment to him is being made by the 
management. 

Uhasbeen held in (19^)4 SCC J lA/'Regularization 
Ad hoc/Temporary govt, employees - Principles laid 
down—Those eligible and qualified and continuing in 
service satisfactorily fur long period have a right to be 
considered tor regularization - Long cofitinuance in service 
gives rise to a presumption about need for a regular post - 
But mere continuance fur one year or so does not in every 
case rabe such a presumption - Govt, should consider 
feasibility of regularization having regard to the particular 
circumstauces with a positive approach and an empathy 
fiff the concerned person." 

It has been held in 1997 AIR SC W Page 430 that the 
industrial ^judicator idionid decide whether there valid 
contract or it is a mere ruse/camouflagc and if it is found 
that Ihe ooniractor is only a name lender the management 
should be directed to regularize the workmefo. In JT 2003 
(1) SC 4d5 the Hon'ble Supreme Court has held that 
industrial adjudication is appropriate remedy for the alleged 
contract workets. 1*1(2000) 1SCC 126 - the Hon^ble Supieme 
Court has held that there are multiple pragmatic approach V 
Actors which should be emuidered in deciding employer 
and employee relationship. According to the criteria there 
should be control and integration. The management has 
doubtless control over the alleged contractor's men as 
they work-in the establishment of the management. They 
are integrated to the service of the management. There are 
no letms and conditions of the contract so there is master 
and servant relationship. The creation of contract labour is 
only sham and camouflage and the employer cannot be 
relieved of his liabilities. 

In Pollock Law of forts a servant and an independent 
contracior has been defined as under ^— 

The distinction between a servant and a independent 
contractorhas been the subject maiter of a large volume of 
case-law from which the text-book writers on torts have 
attempted to lay down some general test$. For example, in 
Pollock's Low of T ofts, (Pages 62 & 63 of Pollock on Torts, 
I $lh £diL) the distinction has thus been broughl out: 

master is one who not only prescribes to the 
workman Ihe end of his woric, but directs or at any moment 
may direct the means also, or, as it has been puk retains the 
powerofcontrcllingthe work, a servant is a person subject 
to the command of his master as to the manner i a which he 


shall do his work...An independent contractor is one who 
undertakes to produce a given result but So that in the 
actual execution ofthe work Is not under the order or control 
of the person for whom he does it, and may use his own 
discretion in things not specified beforehand^^.^.^^, 

in Salmond's Treatise on the Law of Torts the 
distbetion between a servant and independent contractor 
has been indicated as under; 

“What then, is the tcm of this distinction betwe^ ^ 
servant and an independent contractor? The test the 
existence of a right of controt over the agent in respect of 
the manner in which hi^ work is to be done. A servant is an 
agent who works under the supervision and direction of 
his employer; an independeifit contractor is one who is his 
own master A servant is a person engaged to obey his 
employer's orders from time to time; an independent 
contractor is a person engaged to do certain work, but to 
exercise his own discretton as to the mode and time of 
doing it - he is bound by his contract^ but not by his 
employer's orders 

The test regarding independent contractor and 
intennediaiies have been laid down in Hussainabhai^ Calkut 
V. tbe Alath Factory Thczhrlali UniOfO Kos^ikodc [AIR I97S 
SC 1410 (3 Judges)) true lest may, with brevity, be 
indicated once again. Where a worker or group of workers 
labours to produce goods or services and these goods or 
services are for the business of another, that other Is, in 
Fact, the employer. He has economic control over the 
workers subsistence, skill, and continued employment. If 
he, for any reason, chokes off, the worker is, virtually, laid 
off The presence of intermediate contractors with whom 
the workers have immediate or direct relationship as 
contract is of no consequence when, on lifimgthe veil or 
looking at the conspectus of factors governing 
employment, we discern the naked truth, though draped in 
different perfect paper arrangement, that the real employer 
15 the management not the imroediate contractor. Myriad 
devices, halF-hiddeii in fold afler fold of legal form 
depending on the degree of concealment needed, the type 
of industry, the local conditions and the like may be resorted 
to when labour legislation casts wellhre obligations on the 
real employer, based on Articles 38,39,42,43 and 43-Aof 
the Constitution. The Ctiurl must be astute to avoid the 
mischief and achieve the purpose of the law. and not be 
^ misted by the maya of legal appearances." 

ITiLS case Jaw has been affimied by the Constitution 
Bench Judgment in Steel Authority of India. In case the 
security Job chokes ofl, the 'Ax^rkmen would be laid off. 
Such contract is prohibited; it is not a contract For a given 
result.

In Pollock LawofTorts a servant and an independent 
contractor has been defined as under: 

The distinction between a servant atid a independent 
contractor has b«n the subject matter ofa large volume of 
case-law from which the text-t»ok writers on torts have 
aaempted to lay down some general tests, 

per example, in Pollock's Low of TortSt (Pages 62 & 
63 of Pollock on Torts, 15th Edn.) the distinction has thus 
been brought out: 

'‘A master is one who not only prescribes to the 
workman the end of his workplrut directs or at any moment 
may direct the means also, or, as it has been put, retains the 
power of controlling the work, a servant is aperson subject 
to the command of his master as to the mamter in which he 
shall do hb work. An indepondenl contractor is one who 
undertakes to produce a given result but so that in the 
actual execution of the work isiwtundetthe order or control 
of the person for whom he does K and may use his own 
discretion in things not specified betotehand." 

tn Salmond^s Treatise on the Law of Torts the 
disttnctk>n between a servant and independent contractor 
has been indicated as unden 

What then, is the test of this distinction between a 
servant and an independent contructor? The test is the 
existence of a rigjit of control over the agent in respect of 
the manner in which his work is to be done. A servant is an 
agent who works under the supervision and direction of 
hb eniployer; an independent coniractar is one who is his 


own master. A servant is a person engaged to obey his 
employer's orders from time to time; an independent 
contractor is a person engaged to do certain vvorfc* but to 
exetpcire his own discretion as the mode and time ofdouvg it 
he is bound by hb contract, bm ntrt by his employer’s 
orders.^ 

The test regarding independent contraetor and 
interinediaries hove been laid down in Hussatnabhai, Calicut 
V. the AlalhFactOfy Thedtilali Union kozhikoda [(AIR 1978 
SC 1410 (3 Judges)] true test may, with bffivity, be 
indicated once again. Where a worker or group of workers 
labours to produce goods or services and these goods or 
services ore for the business of another, that other b. in 
fact, the employer. He has economic control over the 
workers subsistence, skill, and continued employnient. If 
be, for any reason, chokes off, the worker is, virtually, laid 
oft The presence of mtermediate contractors with whom 
the workers have immediate or direct relationshtp as 
contract is of no consequence when, on lifting the veil or 
looking at the conspectus of factors governing 
employmenr, we discern the naked truth, thou^ draped in 
different perffect paper anrangement, that the real employer 
is the rnauagcmeni, nor the irnTnediate oontractor. Myriad 
devices, half-hidden in fold after fold of legal form 
depending on the degree of concealment needed^ the type 
of industry, the local conditions and the like may be resorted 
to when labour legislation casts welfare obligations on the 
real employer, based onArticLesJS, 39,42,43 and43-Aof 
the Constitution. The Court must be astute to avoid the 
mischief and achieve the purpose of the law and not be 
misled by the maya of legal appearances '" 

This ease law has been affirmed by the Constitution 
Bench Judgment in Steel Authority of India. In case the 
security Job chokes ofF^ the workmen would be laid off. 
Such concract is prohibited; it is not a contract for a given 
result. 

My attention was drawn to another Constitution 
Bench Judgment - Steel Authority of India. It has been 
held asunder:^ 

‘'Where a workman is hired in or in connection with 
the work of an establishment by the principal employer 
through a contractor, he merely acts as an agent so there 
will be master and servant rektiouship between the principal 
employer and the workmen. But where a workman h hired 
in or in connection with the work of on estabtishment by a 
contractor, either because he has undertaken lo produce a 
given result for the establishment or because he supplies 
workmen for any work of the establishment, a question 
miglOC arise whether the contract is a mete camouflage as 
in Hussainabhai Calicut’s case (supra) and in Indian 
Petrochemicals Corporation's case (supra)etc; ifiheanswer 
is in the affirmativa, the workmen will be in fact an employee 
of the prircipal empk>yH-p but if the answer is in die negative, 

the workmen will be a contract labourer.*'

A three Judges bench of the Hon’bte Apex Court

rein^Ulement the status quo ante'of (he workman is

provided in ID Act and it has not been annulled and set 
aside by any judgment of the Hon^ble Apex Court. The 
provisions of the ID Act are still constitutional and they 
are to be given effect too.

In Polfock l^w of Torts a savant Bid an fodepen^ 
ctmtraciCH: has been defined as under :— 

The distinction betAvecn a servant and an 
independent contractor Has been the subject matter of a 
large volume of case-law from which the text-book writers 
on torts have attempted to lay down same general teste- 
For example, in Pollock^E Law of TertSp (Pages 62 &63 of 
Pol lock on TortSp 15th Edn. > the distinction has thus been 
brought out; 

“A master is one Avho not only prescribes to the 
workman the end of his work, but directs or at any 
moment may direct the means also^ or, as it has been 
put, retains the power of controlling the AVOrik, a 
servant is a person subject to the command of his 
master as to the manner in which he shall do hb 
work.„,..,An independent contractor is one who 
undertakes to produce a given result but so that in 
the actual execution of the work is not under the 
order or control of the person for whom he does It, 
and may use hb own discretion in thingj^ not 
specified be forehand.,.." 

In Salmond's Treatise on the Law of Torts the 
distinction betAween a servant and indepetKlent contractor 
has been indicated os under;— 

"'What then, is the test of this distinction beftveen a 
servant and an independent coniractoi? The test is the 
extstence of a right of control over the ageiU iit respect 
of the manner in A^ich his Avork is to be done. A savant 
is an agent who works under the supervision and 
direction ofhis employer, an independait contractor is 
one who is his own master* A servant is a person 
engaged to obey his employer's orders from time to 
time; an independent contractor is a person engaged 
to do certain work, butto exercise hb own discretion as 
to the mode and time of doing it - he is bound hy his 
<OTitract, but not by his employer*s ordes-” 

The lest regarding independent contractor and 
intermediaries have been laiddoAvn inHussainaWiai, Calicut

It was submitted from the side ofthe wodfonsnihst m

ID Act, 1947 has been enacted to safoguard the 
mterest of the workmen belonging to poor segment of 
society. It appears that legislature wanted that such 
worknitei should not be harassed utinecessarily so Sectioii 
25 F, U, T and Clause lOofVlh Schedub have beoi enacted. 
The objects and reasons of ID Act, 1947 show that foe 
respondent management should not be permitted to indulge 
in any unfair labour practice. The workmen should not be 
engaged for years and then they should be removed all of 
a sudden. There is proviston of retienchnKnit compensation 
for his removal. Retrenchment compensation is for 
compensating him otherwise so that he can survive long 
interregnum of unemployment. In the instant case no 
retrendntient compensation has been paid. 

It was submitted foam the side of the management

envisaged by the Constitution. Equality of opportunity is 
the hatimaik and (he Constitution enshrines affirmative 
action to ensure that unequals are not treated equals. So 
public employment should be in tenns of constitutional 
scheme. 

It was fonher submitted that foe Constitution Bench 
Judgment has afforded a right according to which the 
gavemment is not precluded from making temporary 
appoiatments or engaging workers on daily wages. 

Tha government has got no license to make always 
appointment of daily wagers and la continue them for life 
time. Fixed term tenure appointments and temporary 
appointments cannot be the rule of public employment. At 
the time of making temporary appoinlmeiits Articles 14, 
16, 21, 23, 226 and 309 are infringed. There is no 
constitutional mandate that the govemnunt Is at liberty to 
go on giving fixed term appoinnnents for the entire tenure 
of service of an employee. 

The Government or Public Sector units cannot 
continue incessantly to give temporary and fixed term 
appointments again and again. Since fixed term 
appointmems and temporary appointments arc not 
governed by any constitutional scheme, such 
discrimination will amount to vicious discretion. The 
Covenunent or Public Sector unit wlU go on resorting to 
the method of pick and choose policy and give temporary 
and ad hoc appointments to their fovorites and thus the 
principles of equality enshrined in the constitution will be 
given a go bye. Such is not the intent of foe Hon'ble Apex

3. The allegation in the claim statement are briefly as 
follows: 

The petitioner joined the services of the Respondent/ 
Managemeiit on 14'-7-l995 as contract workman Driver. 
Initially he was appointed through a Contiactoa' by name 
Sri RV. Rajagopalan and he was in service continuously 
uplo 16-1-1996. Subsequently, arK^herconttacter by 
Sri P. Thangavel employed the petitioner as a driver in the 
mamienance and project depaitment of the Respondent. 
The petitiooer worked continuously without any break, 
whatsoever, since the date of joining even though 
contractors have been changing every year or so. The 
work performed by the petitioner is perennial in nature and 
the so-called contract between the Respondent and the 
respective contractors are mere paper artangement The 
work perfbimed by the petitioner was directly cootrolled 
and supervised by the Officers of the Respondent/ 
Management and the so called contractors hive no role to 
play except tending their names as contractors. Even the 
payments are made directly by the RespondenL Thus^ the 
so-calfed contract between Respondent end the contractor 
are only pretence, nofninal and sham and a make believe 
arrangement. The contract is a mere ruse and camouflage 
to evnde compllance of various beneficial legislations and 
in the matter of payment of wages. The Respondent is 
adopting the device of contract In order to deny the 
petitioner the status of regular workman of the Rjespondent 
and the benefits available to the workmen dirocOy einplcyed 
by him. Thereffire, he has filed a Writ Petition before the 
HcwVbte High Court of Madras in WPNo. l9442Wto absorb 
him and rvgul^i» his services from the date of his initial 
appointment. While the matter was pending before the High 
Courts the Respondent had terminated the services of the 
petitioner without foLlowing the mandatory requirement of 
Section 25(F) of the Industrial Disputes Act and the WP 
was disposed ofby an order dated 27-^-2003 giving liberty
